Audit record for the completion workflow (`CLAUDE.md`, amended 2026-08-12 / -08-14; standing
rules in `docs/architecture/COMPLETION_WORKFLOW_RULES.md`).

Plan of record: `.codex/plans/issue-155.md` (the attested architect plan, plus the folded
2026-08-25 addendum and the corrections that override it). The architect gate attested it at sha256
`d60496a6fb2083f9c371deae8dc84009051aa44551204fe157aa45b3fee00f0d`; attestation
`.codex/plans/issue-155.attest.json`. On 2026-08-27 the owner AMENDED it, appending a dated section
adopting the maintained evidence archiver as in-scope, which moves the file to sha256
`3020c7c2d40bc23a2c8b3b10d6d2155b09b6b4bf42f41f416bf30e666df0eb2f`. Both digests are recorded so the
attested bytes stay identifiable: the attestation covers the plan as it stood BEFORE the amendment,
and the amendment adds scope without revoking a contract, gate or acceptance criterion. The landing strategy this ledger executes lives at
`docs/plans/issue-155-strategy.md` (local-only, like every plan file here).

## Slice C — Stage-1 step 0 — baseline

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Literal baseline SHA | `eb2235144400c80a13c36d70244740eb38929be5` |
| Branch point | `dev` @ `eb22351` (slice B's landed tip) |
| Baseline suite | 11,065 tests collected, tree clean |
| Slice kind | behaviour-affecting — trusted connector-resolution snapshot + blank-path net (plan §5.C, U5 + S11) |
| Live account | `trainingglebbochkarov-16926N`, rebuilt 2026-08-28 per the §9 runbook; O14 discharged |
| Trust boundary | CREATES the snapshot module and its diagnostics; CONSUMES `_resolve_existing_components`, the connector builders, the symbol table and the four write sinks |

**Recorded at step 0, before any code — two of the plan's own prescriptions carry measured
traps.** §5.C says to rewrite the two symbol-table reconstruction helpers as
`model_copy(update=…)`. Measured on this tree, that instruction is unsafe in two independent
ways, and both were checked by running them rather than by reading pydantic's docs:

1. `model_copy(update=…)` does NOT re-run field validators. `SymbolTableV1` canonicalises by
   sorting on `ref` at construction precisely so a caller's insertion order cannot reach
   compiler output; a rebuild via `model_copy` carries that order straight through. Probe:
   the constructor yields `['$ref:a','$ref:b']` where `model_copy` yields `['$ref:b','$ref:a']`.
2. `model_copy(update=…)` does NOT honour `extra="forbid"`. The constructor rejects an unknown
   key; `model_copy` accepts it and sets a real attribute that `model_fields` does not list.
   So the prescribed fix trades a silent DROP for a silent PHANTOM.

The applied fix therefore derives the carried field set from `model_fields` and goes through the
CONSTRUCTOR — `SymbolTableV1.rebinding(source, symbols)` — which keeps the canonicalising sort,
keeps `extra="forbid"`, and cannot drop a field nobody named. Recorded here rather than silently
diverging from the plan: the plan's goal (stop hand-enumerating) is right, its mechanism is not.

### Slice C — implementation record (units landed so far)

**Unit 1 — the symbol-table rebind is derived.** `SymbolTableV1.rebinding(source, symbols)`
carries every field but `symbols`, derived from `model_fields`, through the CONSTRUCTOR. Both
reconstruction helpers route through it and neither names a carried field any more. Four
witnesses; graded by reverting a helper to the enumerated form, which fails.

**Unit 2 — the pre-apply identity projection.** `normalized_identity_projection(config,
live_projection=None)` returns family, action and route state, credential-free by allowlist. It
never calls `build()` — checked over the AST, because the first version of that check grepped
for `.build(` and matched the function's own docstring saying it does not call build; a scan
that cannot tell prose from a call proves nothing about either.

**Two fail-opens in unit 2 were found by PROBING the function, not by reading it**, and both are
recorded because each would have minted an identity that cannot exist:

1. A `path_replacements` declaration that is PRESENT but malformed made the first version answer
   `route_state="static"` and mintable. `validate_config` refuses such a config outright, so no
   component is ever built from it. It now answers `unavailable`.
2. The database family reported itself mintable with `endpoint=None`, because the projection read
   only the URL keys and that family pins its route with discrete `host`/`port`/`dbname` fields.
   Mintability now requires a route to have actually been read — a route nobody read is not a
   route anybody knows.

**Recorded limitation, asserted rather than described.** The endpoint is reduced by the same
credential-free skeleton rule the reuse surface applies, so a base-URL PATH (a webhook token, a
session id) never reaches the projection. Consequence: two connections differing ONLY in
base-URL path project identically here. That is deliberate — this is a pre-apply identity, not
the route digest, which reads the applied component's XML and keeps the full path. A test pins
the consequence so it cannot drift silently.

**Unit 3 — the four symbol-table sinks became three.** The apply path
(`_execute_canonical_process`) hand-copied `_build_canonical_symbols` with byte-identical
arguments from 280 lines below it, so threading anything into the canonical symbol table had two
places to reach and would have reached one. That is `DC-155-B`, a second builder of an
already-resolved fact, and it was visible in the tree before this slice touched it: the helper's
docstring claimed "ONE construction, three callers" while only two callers used it. Apply now
calls the helper — three callers, as the docstring always said — and the two local imports that
existed only for the hand copy are gone. An AST guard pins that the table is constructed nowhere
but inside that helper. Graded BOTH directions, which the first version was not: it forbade every
direct call including the helper's own and therefore failed on a clean tree — a guard that cannot
pass is not a guard.

**Unit 4 — the trusted resolution snapshot, and the reach it does NOT yet have.**
`authoring/connector_resolution_snapshot.py` resolves each connector component from its own
config and compares the caller's declaration against it, so the declaration becomes an assertion
rather than an override. Both `CONNECTOR_REPLAY_IDENTITY_MISMATCH` is registered and RAISED — the taxonomy's own guard
refuses a published code nothing can produce, which is the rule this repository already applies
to compiler diagnostics, and it fired on the first attempt to register them without a raiser.
Neither code is named under `compiler/process_ir/`: a code imported there joins the compiler's
published surface, and connector identity is an account fact.

**A fail-CLOSED bug, caught by probing the wiring rather than by the suite.** The declared half
carries the RAW connector type (`rest_client`) and the resolved half a canonical family (`rest`),
so the first version compared every REST component unequal to ITSELF and refused — including the
control. Both sides now go through one derivation, `connector_family_of`.

**RECORDED LIMITATION, measured and pinned: at this sink the comparison is TAUTOLOGICAL.** At
`_build_canonical_symbols` both halves are computed from the same `spec.components` — the
declared half by `_connector_metadata_from_components`, the resolved half by the projection — and
for every family they read the same config keys. Probed across the shapes that ought to disagree
(`method` versus `action_type` in both directions, and the database equivalent): the guard fires
on NONE of them, 0 of 4. So this is real machinery wired where it cannot yet trigger.

That is stated plainly rather than left to be discovered, because a guard that cannot fire is the
defect class this slice has spent its whole review history on. The declaration only becomes
independent evidence when it comes from a source OTHER than the config — a live component read
back for reuse, or the requirements-derived producer on the recipe route — and supplying that is
the next unit. A test pins the tautology and FAILS the moment the sink gains a real
disagreement, so the limitation cannot go stale silently.

**Rebaseline performed:** the new module moves the M12.12 census 226 -> 227 sources; the
inventory fixture and the two §11 tables that carry it were regenerated from the same run. The
first attempt spliced the emit's `[INFO]` log lines into the tracked document and destroyed a
section heading (39 -> 38) — the same log-contamination that produced two bogus manifest rows
earlier in this issue, repeated. Reverted and redone by replacing only the two drifted TABLES:
6 insertions, 6 deletions, headings intact.

**Unit 5 — the tautology is CLOSED.** `live_identity_from_component_xml` derives family and
action from the component as the ACCOUNT stores it, and the snapshot prefers that reading over
the request's config wherever it is supplied. That is what makes the comparison independent
evidence: a declaration checked against the config it was derived from cannot disagree, and the
0-of-4 measurement recorded above is what that looks like. The refusal now names its source, so
"the component stored in the account" and "its own configuration" are distinguishable in the
message rather than only in the code.

Verified against the SIX archived verb captures — `cap155-e2-post`, `-put`, `-delete`, `-head`,
`-options`, `-trace`. Those are readbacks of components the platform actually served, not
fixtures written to match the reader, which is the only direction that proves the reader right;
the fixture-provenance rule this workflow adds at 2026-08-14 asks for exactly that. The
declared-GET-over-an-account-POST case now REFUSES with
`CONNECTOR_REPLAY_IDENTITY_MISMATCH`, and two controls keep it honest: the same live reading
ACCEPTS a declaration that agrees with it, and with the live XML withheld the config's own GET
resolves and the declaration agrees — so the refusal is caused by the live reading and nothing
else.

**Still owed for U5:** the apply path does not yet READ that XML. The comparison is capable and
proven, and the sink that will feed it (`_execute_canonical_process`, which holds the Boomi
client) is identified; supplying it is the next unit, and until then the live half is exercised
by tests rather than by a running apply. Named here rather than implied.

**Unit 6 — apply reads the account, so U5's comparison is now live.** `_live_connector_xml`
fetches stored XML for every connector component the request REUSES, and the apply-time symbol
table is built with it. Apply is the first point that HAS an account to read, and the read still
happens before this root's first write, so a mismatch refuses rather than being discovered after
a mutation.

Three properties, each with its own test rather than asserted together:

* **Only reused components are read.** A component being CREATED has no account-side truth to
  disagree with, so reading nothing for it is correct rather than merely cheap — and it means a
  create-only apply adds ZERO platform calls, which is what the `Q10` call-count probe will
  measure.
* **An unreadable component skips the comparison.** A transient platform error must not become
  an authoring refusal; that is the same "I could not tell is not evidence" rule already applied
  to an extension-bound endpoint.
* **The refusal is caused by the account and nothing else.** The end-to-end test refuses a
  declared GET over a stored POST through `_build_canonical_symbols`, and its control builds the
  IDENTICAL spec cleanly with the live reading withheld.

With this, U5's declared-vs-derived comparison is no longer a tautology in the tree, only in the
one sink where both halves still come from the config. The `0 of 4` measurement recorded above
remains true of the PLAN path and is now a statement about where the evidence comes from rather
than an unqualified limitation.

**Unit 7 — `requires_path_binding` and the blank-path refusal (S11).** The symbol table gains a
FOURTH optional field, and the tracked architecture note that says it "gained exactly three" is
amended rather than left to contradict the code. That note already reasons about two proposed
fields and rejects both, so it sets the test this one had to pass, and it was checked rather than
argued:

* Not a second encoding — the capability allowlist carries `(family, action)` pairs and says
  nothing about a stored path; `binding_variant` was rejected because it WAS such an encoding.
* Not inert — the snapshot populates it and the refusal consumes it; `dependency_refs` was
  rejected because nothing populated it.
* Not derivable here — the operation's stored path lives in the component, and reading that
  would break the purity this layer promises, so the fact must arrive from outside.

Tri-state on purpose. `None` means nobody told the compiler, which is the common case, and only
an explicit `True` refuses: reading silence as "does not require one" is fail-open, reading it as
"does" would refuse every document that never mentioned a path.

`validate_dynamic_path_required` sits INSIDE `validate_connector_calls` — the module's own rule
is that a gate only one of two entry points enforces is not a gate, and an AST test pins that it
is reached from there. Both halves graded by mutation: neutering the refusal fails the witness,
and stopping the population makes the field read `None`, which is exactly the inert state the
architecture note rejects.

**Rebaselines, each reviewed rather than accepted:** the taxonomy test's pinned per-issue code set gains the new code; the
served authoring contract was regenerated and its diff INSPECTED — exactly one diagnostic code
added, none removed, which is what a new refusal should do to text an LLM caller reads; the
M12.12 census and five served digests moved and were re-emitted, replacing only the two drifted
tables so the document's 39 headings survived.

of the five was a defect no unit test in this slice could have found, which is the argument for
the public-boundary gate rather than a fact about this round. They are recorded at

The two worth carrying forward as lessons rather than as rows:

* **`QA-155-r42-02` is the SECOND composition defect in this slice.** Units 5 and 7 were each
  correct and each tested; the live reading answered `static` for every component, which
  DISARMED the blank-path net exactly where it matters — a reused operation the account stores
  with a blank path. A process was applied whose connector action carried no Path property. The
  tautology was the first of these, and both times the halves passed while the whole failed.
* **`QA-155-r42-03` shows a guard I trusted was satisfied by my own test.** The taxonomy's

**A ledger-discipline slip, recorded because the record is the thing it damages.** Renumbering
these rows off a colliding id, a global string replace also renamed slice A's byte-frozen

are numbered continuously across slices in this ledger, which is what made `r1` collide in the

**Stage-1 QA round 2 — all five round-1 findings confirmed fixed; four new, none critical.**
The mutation-accounting fix was confirmed THREE independent ways, which is what I had asked for
because a refusal moving earlier can look right in the code and still leave a write ahead of it:
the response envelope, a platform query returning ZERO rows rather than a deleted one, and zero
component POSTs at the wire.

**The finding that matters is `QA-155-r43-01`, and it is mine twice over.** Fixing the critical
introduced it: adding an `unavailable` route state gave the `resolved` predicate a new way to be
false, and the identity comparison gated on that predicate — so a correction to the PATH question
silently disabled the VERB question, and a component the account stores as a PATCH could be
declared a GET and applied. One gate had been standing for two independent facts.

**Two answers the round settled that were open questions, not defects.** `plan` REPORTS the
blank-path refusal rather than refusing — `_success` stays true with the verdict in the validation
report — which is planning's stated contract of handing a caller everything wrong at once. And the
reference-only shape cannot express a bypass at all: a connector call needs an allowlisted family
and action read from the declared config, so a declaration too thin to assert anything is too thin
to compile. My round-1 probe for that had measured the dry-run flag instead, and the agent said so
rather than reporting a pass.

**A code was WITHDRAWN rather than given an invented consumer.** The unavailability companion was
registered while its only caller was a test — the published-code-nothing-can-produce this taxonomy
already refuses elsewhere. It returns with its raiser, on the no-client reuse path this slice has
not built.

**Stage-1 QA round 3 — all four cells pass; ONE new finding, Low, fixed.** Stage-1 for slice C
is CLEAN: three rounds, ten raw findings, every one dispositioned.

The round-3 evidence for `QA-155-r43-01` is the strongest in this slice and worth naming for
reuse: two components differing by exactly ONE element — a platform readback with the single
`<field id="path" …/>` removed, re-POSTed and re-read to confirm the removal — plus a third arm
declaring the AGREEING verb against that same damaged component. The third arm is what settles it:
it APPLIES, so the refusal in the second is the verb mismatch and not the damage. A two-arm
differential could not have told those apart.

**`QA-155-r44-01` is a pattern, not an incident.** I accepted the dry-run limitation one round
earlier and wrote it down HERE — and a limitation recorded only where the implementer reads it is
not disclosed to anyone who has to live with it. The dry-run envelope now says what it does not
check. The QA agent also volunteered that the reasoning for not moving the live read was right,
rather than only reporting the gap, which is the more useful half of that exchange.

**Stage-1 QA disposition for slice C:** round 1 five findings (one critical, three high, one
medium) all fixed; round 2 four findings (one high, one medium, two low) — three fixed, one
recorded as a bounded limitation; round 3 one finding, fixed. Two of the highs and the critical
were defects the unit tests could not have found, and two were holes OPENED by an earlier fix in

**Not yet wired.** Units 1 and 2 are consumed by nothing outside their tests. That is the
`#180` inertness shape and it is named here rather than left implicit: the next unit is the
snapshot module plus its first real consumer, and neither of these lands on `dev` before that

## Artifact trust boundary

The slice CREATES AND OWNS: the canonical path binding and its semantic mirrors, the family
capability authority and its published projection, the replay-evidence package and its derived
report, the resolution snapshot, the grant symbols, the attestation tuple, the issue-155 goldens
and fixtures (regression pins, never their own oracle), this ledger and its evidence archive, and
its own entries in the wave-gate test module. It CONSUMES, and never re-authors: the legacy
process-flow builder and shape renderer (the parity oracle), the Boomi platform readbacks and
execution records, the sandbox counterparty, and the committed wave-gate manifests. A finding that
only hardens a self-owned artifact gets a boundary verdict at reconciliation instead of a fix batch
by default.

### Pre-committed stopping rule for the evidence archiver (recorded BEFORE the round it governs)

Rounds 5, 6 and 7 of the Stage-2 loop all landed on one artifact — the archiver this slice had to
write because no producer for the archive index existed — and nothing they found reaches a served
surface. That artifact is FINITE; the space of malformed attestations, mis-supplied paths and
foreign index shapes it could be asked to survive is UNBOUNDED. This repository has twice recorded
what that costs: a finite artifact bound to an unbounded space does not converge, and the rounds
keep finding correct defects until someone states a rule. Stated here before round 8 opens, so it
is a rule and not a rationalisation of whatever that round returns.

From round 8, each archiver finding is judged into exactly one bucket at reconciliation:

1. It can damage something this slice does not own — another issue's archive, a committed record, a
   gate's evidence, the tree. FIX IT, and note that its blocking class is mutation accounting, which
   derives critical on its own anchor and is therefore never deferrable. Round 7's re-derivation
   finding and the self-recorded verdict-erasure are both this bucket.
2. It lets the archiver accept evidence that is WRONG — a wrong prompt, an unattested round, a null
   digest where a real one exists, a completion the collector declined to record. FIX IT. This is
   the artifact's entire purpose; an archiver that accepts bad evidence is not unhardened, it is
   broken. Rounds 5 and 6 were this bucket.
3. It makes the archiver crash or misbehave on input NO producer emits. RECORD A BOUNDARY VERDICT:
   name the input, name the producer that would have to emit it, and stop. These earn a row and a
   disposition, not a code change. The archiver's inputs come from two known collectors and from
   this script itself, and a defect in what those emit is a defect in them.

Round 7's non-string-digest finding sits on the 2/3 line and was fixed rather than verdicted only
because the fix was one clause of what bucket 1 already required. A standalone successor of that
shape is bucket 3. This rule bounds which findings earn a CODE change and relaxes nothing else: not
owed validation, not the undeferrability of a critical finding, not the wave gate's currency on the
final tree, and not the recording rules — every finding still gets a row and exactly one
disposition.

### Archiver FREEZE, declared before round 13 (recorded 2026-08-27)

The stopping rule above bounded which findings earn a code change. It was not enough, and the
measurement says so plainly. Rounds 5 through 12 — EIGHT consecutive Stage-2 evaluations, seventeen
findings — landed entirely on the evidence archiver. Not one reached a served surface; slice A's
functional code has returned clean since round 4. Across those rounds I introduced a regression or
reintroduced a closed defect class into that artifact repeatedly, and round 12 recorded the
check-after-mutate shape arriving for the THIRD time, twice after a structural fix meant to end it.

Three structural fixes each did end their own line of findings — deriving the row from the consuming
authority, publishing the round by an atomic move, bounding the names the manifest can record. New
lines kept opening. That is not a converging loop, and the honest reading is that continuing to
harden this artifact inside this slice costs more than it protects.

**So the archiver is FROZEN for slice A from round 13.** The artifact has done its job: twelve rounds
are archived, the index is derived and verified, and the repository's own scanners pass on it. From
here a STANDARD-tier finding against it gets a row, a disposition and a boundary verdict naming the
mechanism, and is surfaced to the owner as residue rather than fixed.

**Corrected at its first application (round 13).** As first written this rule said a code change
required a finding to be BOTH critical AND reachable through the invocation this loop actually
makes. That is wrong, and applying it once was enough to show why: the completion workflow states
that a critical finding is never deferrable — fix and validate, or escalate with the issue open —
and a local scope decision of mine cannot override the governing rule. A freeze that would have
boundary-verdicted a critical finding was not a stricter rule, it was an unauthorised waiver.

So the freeze binds STANDARD residue only. A critical finding against this artifact is still fixed
and validated, however narrow its trigger, or the issue escalates open. Round 13's finding is
exactly that case: labelled P1 by its gate, in the mutation-accounting class, and therefore critical
by two independent anchors — so it was fixed, and the freeze did not and could not excuse it. What
the freeze still does is stop the loop spending itself on the standard-tier hardening that consumed
rounds 5 through 12. Recorded here rather than quietly re-scoped, because a rule corrected on
contact with the case it was written for should say so.

This is a decision about SCOPE, not about validation. It relaxes nothing: the round-12 correction
still owes its affected QA and its delta-scoped review, a critical finding remains undeferrable,
the wave gate must still be current on the final tree, and every finding still gets exactly one
recorded disposition. What it stops is the loop treating a self-owned tool as though it were the
product. The residue is real and is recorded as such; filing it is the owner's to authorise, and
this loop does not file.

## Loop roster (fixed BEFORE the first correction; identical shape per slice, FRESH windows each)

A gate not on this list cannot mint a loop mid-run; adding one is itself a recorded checkpoint
decision.

1. Stage-1 QA — live scoped pass through the public MCP tool boundary (`boomi-qa-tester`)
2. Stage-2 repo Codex commit review — delta-scoped; every round COLLECTED, never read from a poll
3. Architect implementation review — FIXED CAP of three evaluations per
   `docs/architecture/COMPLETION_WORKFLOW_RULES.md`; the prompt inlines the plan of record and is
   scoped to the slice's units, and an absence finding for a later slice's unit is refuted citing
   the slice map
4. Composite wave gate — full suite, every active golden, determinism, manifests; its passing SHA
   is named in the checkpoint row
5. Closing protocol — the record-only commits after the wave gate, validated by a darkness proof
   and the ledger scanners; a terminal correction loop exists only via a recorded roster addition

Evidence-capture dispatches, polls, approvals, claim-only re-asks and the advisory image-parity
workflow are not evaluations.

STRUCTURAL FIX, both halves: the settlement question now CALLS the composer rather than inspecting the fields it reads, and the bindability question is answered by a table already pinned as a subset of the builder's placeholder sets, with a test that consults `DatabaseConnectorBuilder.validate_config` DIRECTLY as its oracle and derives the expected answer per field from it. SIBLING SWEEP over the projection: the action derivation is pinned to the derivation it mirrors, the route branch consults the capability table, the family field lists are pinned to the placeholder sets, and the composer declares the fields it reads — no predicate in this function now restates a rule its owner could be asked for. NON-VACUITY: a mutant restoring the presence test fails the settlement case; a mutant folding the composed fields back into the binding claim fails the bindability case, whose expectation is computed from the validator rather than written down. COVERAGE: the three database fields are the validator's own case set for this family, enumerated by asking it |

Second-instance check runs at row-write time and whenever a second finding lands in the same
file or subsystem within a loop.

**Cross-authority recurrence recorded at this reconciliation (DC-155-A, instances eight and nine).**
The sibling sweep found two more sites with the same mechanism — a per-family fact expressed as a
chain of conditionals rather than read from a table — in the snapshot reader rather than in the
projection: the per-family verb location, which sits beside two tables that already key on family,
and the blank-path route gate, whose family test is hardcoded where the connector family capability
table is the authority that already answers it. Both are the same mechanism under a DIFFERENT
runtime authority, so the structural-fix rule makes them a recorded recurrence plus a follow-up
filed at reconciliation — never a same-slice cross-subsystem refactor. The follow-up is NOT filed:
issue filing in this repository is the owner's to authorize, and the standing pre-authorization
names a third instance of the hand-listed-enumeration class as its trigger, which is a different
class from this one. Surfaced to the owner instead, with the two sites named.

**A correction to how this table is maintained, found while updating it.** The instance lists are
CURATED, not derivable by scanning the rows for a class identifier: several early rows name their
class in prose rather than by label, so a derive-by-label pass silently drops them. A first attempt
at re-deriving one list would have deleted two genuine instances while presenting the result as a
derivation. The lists are therefore appended to, and only the COUNT is computed — from the list
itself, never typed. This is the same overclaim the round's own low-severity finding names, one
level up.

Corrected and verified independently: the served specification types this field as a string with a minimum length, and the canonical model REJECTS a bare integer — so the code was emitting an integer where its own served schema declares a string, and the coercion makes the payload CONFORM rather than changing what it promises |

Because the account is alive for one more day this is cheap now and impossible afterwards, so the missing conjunct is being captured directly from the counterparty rather than reasoned around. The three write verbs are unaffected: each is admissible on a MEASURED state change at the targeted resource, which no refusal could have produced |
| SELF-155-r3-08a | Revision of `SELF-155-r3-08` (original retained, byte-frozen), recorded when the capture it committed to returned | Same gap: the three read verbs lacked the status half of a two-conjunct admissibility rule | none (self-recorded evidence-sufficiency gap) | capability reachability | unchanged | standard — UNCHANGED | `a0463e6` | fixed — CLOSED BY MEASUREMENT. Stage-1 QA evidence-capture dispatch E4, archives `captures/cap155-e4-head-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-options-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-trace-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-negative-control`. Each capture records the counterparty's own log line for the request the platform issued, and each verb appears EXACTLY ONCE in its capture's log, which is what makes the attribution sound rather than adjacent-in-time: a success for the existence check, a no-content for the allowed-methods query, and a success for the loopback verb. The readback half was already measured and is unchanged in all three. The rule's two conjuncts are now both evidenced, so the three read verbs move from inferred to attested. The negative control is what makes this non-vacuous rather than a set of agreeable numbers: the same instrument, pointed at a method the counterparty refuses, records the refusal in the log WHILE that execution still reads complete with zero inbound errors and its connector record still reads success — verified by reading both artifacts directly, not from the report. That reproduces the platform's blindness on today's account and runtime rather than only in the archived earlier evidence, and proves the instrument reads the counterparty |
| QA-155-r28-01 | Stage-1 QA evidence-capture dispatch E4, archives `captures/cap155-e4-head-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-options-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-trace-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-negative-control` | The allowed-methods response header is not a success signal: the refusal control's connector record carries it too | Low | none (an admissibility inference, not code) | a header signature read as a status | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Low; nothing shipped, and the inference it refutes was never relied on | `a0463e6` | fixed — the inference is WITHDRAWN, and this row exists because I nearly relied on it. The audit that found the gap offered this header as the strongest available substitute for the missing status, on the strength of its value matching the counterparty's declared method list. Measured, the refusal control carries the same header, so it distinguishes nothing. The correct evidence is the counterparty's log line, which is what was captured. A signature that correlates with the answer on the cases you looked at is not the answer |
| QA-155-r28-02 | Stage-1 QA evidence-capture dispatch E4, archives `captures/cap155-e4-head-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-options-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-trace-status`, `captures/cap155-e4-negative-control` | The document-download action serves one identical status for a success, a no-content and a refusal alike; it is the platform's own status for the artifact request, and that meaning is undocumented for the action | Low | machine-served schemas/contracts | a transport-level status mistaken for the payload's status | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Low; the value is correct for what it describes and no caller in this tree reads it as a verb outcome | `a0463e6` | not-validated as a defect in this slice, and recorded rather than fixed. The value is accurate for what it actually reports — whether the platform accepted the request for the artifact — so nothing served is wrong; what is missing is that the action's text never says so, which invites exactly the misreading this slice made once already. A connector-field census across all twelve captured records confirms no status field exists anywhere in them, so there is no alternative field a caller could have meant. The remedy is served text on an action this slice does not otherwise touch, and inventing that edit here would be scope with no consumer; it is recorded so the next reader of those captures does not repeat the misreading |
| EVAL-155-11 | self, measured at slice B's step 0 from the archived captures (no gate run) | The platform's execution-connector rows report the same action value for every HTTP verb. Across 95 rows in 16 capture directories spanning eight verbs, the vocabulary is exactly three pairs: the real connector with a single generic execute action, plus the no-data and return sentinels. The verb is NOT in the row — it lives in the operation component's own method field | none (self-recorded measurement that settles a plan-flagged unknown) | machine-served schemas/contracts | none | standard — anchor: no defect; a measurement recorded before the slice that depends on it | `6d2205a` | fixed — the plan flagged this exact fact as unknowable without live captures and required it be measured before slice B authored its vocabulary mapping. Measured now, and it CHANGES the design: a mapping keyed on the execution-connector action would be total but useless, collapsing all eight verbs onto one value, so the verb must be read from the operation component instead. Recording it here rather than discovering it in review is the whole point of the rule. The measurement also removes slice B's last account dependency — its allowlist input is fully banked across every verb, so B is unaffected by the account expiring |
| EVAL-155-12 | self, measured at slice B while designing the ingest reader (no gate run) | The plan's ingest contract names a per-scenario manifest file that exists in NO capture directory, and assumes one schema for the sidecar that does exist. Measured: zero files of the named kind across 31 directories, and the sidecar that is actually present carries EIGHT distinct shapes — one object form in twelve directories and six different list forms elsewhere, with key sets that agree on almost nothing | none (self-recorded measurement that contradicts a plan contract) | machine-served schemas/contracts | a reader modelled on a hand-written sidecar whose shape drifted per capture generation | standard — anchor: nothing shipped; found while designing the reader, which is when it is cheapest | `8d58f51` | fixed by CHANGING THE AUTHORITY rather than by teaching the reader eight shapes. Modelling all eight would be the hand-list defect this slice has already paid for repeatedly, and a ninth generation would break it again. The sidecar is provenance written by the capture tooling; the EVIDENCE is the platform's own artifacts beside it — the execution record, the connector record, the readback delta and the counterparty log — whose shapes the platform defines and does not drift. Integrity likewise comes from the archive's single top-level checksum manifest, which covers every file uniformly, rather than from per-record digest fields that only some generations carry. So the reader derives what it needs from the artifacts and refuses a capture that cannot supply them, and the sidecar's shape stops mattering. The plan text is superseded on this point and the correction is recorded here rather than left to surface as a review finding |
| SELF-155-r27-01 | the local attestation guard fired during slice B; investigated rather than dismissed (no gate run) | The guard reported a run directory whose teardown claimed the daemon was confirmed stopped while the daemon was alive — a contradiction it is built to refuse. Measured: the daemon was NOT alive. The directory was three days old, its socket file was gone, the driver's own inventory reported no live daemons, and no matching process existed. The guard asks whether the recorded process id answers, and process ids are RECYCLED, so a stale record eventually names an unrelated process | none (a local guard's precision, not shipped code) | none — the tracked in-tree scanner does not use process liveness; it verifies the durable archive, so the authoritative check was never affected | a liveness probe that models a daemon by a number the operating system reuses | standard — anchor: no orphaned daemon existed, no attestation was wrong, and nothing served or archived is affected | `da49bdc` | fixed — the probe now requires BOTH that the recorded process answers AND that the socket it listened on still exists, because the daemon removes its socket on teardown. Proven by four constructed cases rather than by reading: a live process with its socket still reports alive, a live process whose socket has vanished no longer does, a dead process reports dead, and a record with no socket falls back to the process check rather than reporting clean — an unknown state must never degrade into a clean one. The offending directory was removed only after its teardown was verified and four independent probes agreed the daemon was gone. Worth recording because a guard that cries wolf is worse than no guard: the response to its next firing becomes 'probably the false positive again', which is how a real orphan would get waved through |
| QA-155-r27-01 | Stage-1 QA slice B round 1, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-sliceB-r1.md` | The new monitoring action is advertised on no discovery surface, and the capability catalog regressed from complete to incomplete — measured 17 of 17 at the slice baseline and 17 of 18 at the slice tip | High | machine-served schemas/contracts | a hand-listed enumeration of actions whose authority is the router's dispatch table | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it High, not P0/P1/Critical; no blocking-class anchor forces critical, and nothing executes wrong — the capability is reachable, only undiscoverable | `bcdbf2b` | fixed — and the finding is that MY STRUCTURAL FIX WAS INCOMPLETE, which is worse than not having attempted one. The defect class was already at nine instances, so an instance patch was not available: the rule required a sibling sweep over every instance of the mechanism in the affected artifacts. I derived ONE copy — the router's own error envelope — and left two, so adding an action silently degraded a surface that had been complete. The plan named all four copies before work started; I read it and swept one. Now swept properly: the discovery catalog is derived from the dispatch table, verified by a mutation control rather than by reading. The tool docstring cannot be derived — it is captured at decoration time — so it gets DETECTION instead: a test asserts it names every dispatchable action and fails loudly when one ships undocumented. That test found seven further gaps that predate this slice, and they are fixed in the same pass rather than frozen as debt |
| QA-155-r27-02 | Stage-1 QA slice B round 1, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-sliceB-r1.md` | The exported set of execution sentinels has no consumers, and its one would-be consumer re-lists the same two values inline | Medium | machine-served schemas/contracts | the same hand-listed enumeration, one level down — a constant created to stop a re-listing, beside a re-listing | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Medium; the two spellings agreed, so nothing served was wrong | `bcdbf2b` | fixed — both the model validator and the monitoring handler now read the exported set. | QA-155-r27-03 | Stage-1 QA slice B round 1, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-sliceB-r1.md` | The registry loader reads two top-level keys and ignores the rest, including one its own packaged file ships | Medium | machine-served schemas/contracts | a reader that silently narrows the contract its data declares | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Medium; the dropped key is empty in this slice, so nothing served is currently wrong | `bcdbf2b` | fixed — the key is read and exposed even though it is always empty here, because the alternative is a key the file declares and the reader cannot see, which is exactly how the drop went unnoticed. The loader now also REFUSES a top-level key it does not understand rather than ignoring it: a key present in the data and absent from the reader is a disagreement, not a default, and the rows this registry will one day carry are the ones that decide whether a write may be retried |
| QA-155-r27-04 | Stage-1 QA slice B round 1, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-sliceB-r1.md` | The one field identifying WHICH connector ran is dropped from the served row | Medium | capability reachability | a projection that omits the field its own purpose statement names | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Medium; the rows are served and correct, but incomplete for their stated use | `bcdbf2b` | fixed — the field is served. The handler's own docstring said these rows exist to say WHICH connector ran, and the projection did not carry that. A reader could learn a REST connector ran and how much it moved, but not which one, which is the attribution the later ingest depends on |
| QA-155-r27-05 | Stage-1 QA slice B round 1, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-sliceB-r1.md` | The unset-value normaliser's sentinel branch is unreachable, its docstring asserts something false about the generated client, and its test constructs a state that client cannot produce | Low | none (an internal helper's docstring and its test) | a fixture asserting a property of a dependency, written from belief rather than measured | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Low; nothing served is wrong and the branch is harmless | `bcdbf2b` | fixed — this is the sharpest finding in the round and its rating understates it. The client names its sentinel as a parameter DEFAULT but assigns nothing for an omitted argument, so a fresh record's attribute lookup yields nothing rather than the sentinel; across every live record captured for this work, zero attributes held one. My test looked like coverage because it planted a sentinel by hand — a state the client cannot reach — so a passing test certified a path that does not exist. The fixture is now a REAL client record constructed with no arguments, the docstring states the measurement instead of the belief and says plainly that the branch is defensive, and a separate test pins the client's behaviour so that if it ever changes, the branch becoming load-bearing is reported rather than discovered in a served payload. This is the same lesson this repository has already recorded once: a fixture you wrote is not evidence |
| QA-155-r27-06 | Stage-1 QA slice B round 1, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-sliceB-r1.md` | A nonexistent execution is served identically to one whose connector rows have not yet materialised | Low | runtime behavior | a success envelope whose emptiness has two causes and names neither | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it Low; the response is accurate, merely incomplete | `bcdbf2b` | fixed — an empty result now states that it cannot distinguish its two causes and points at the action that can. Not fixed by adding an existence probe: that would spend a platform call on every empty result to answer a question the caller may not be asking, and the honest sentence costs nothing |

| CDX-155-r23-01 | Stage-2 Codex commit review round 2 (fix-only, slice B), run `cdx-review.3kTSub`, archived `commit-reviews/cdx-review.3kTSub`, reviewed `6dde1c2` | The previous round's fix reads the wrong attribute from the custom-property elements, so populated query parameters and request headers still contribute nothing to the configuration digest, and the new test misses it because it constructs a shape the system never emits | P1 | machine-served schemas/contracts | a fixture written from belief about a dependency's shape rather than from the producer of that shape | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it P1, not P0/Critical; the digest is computed by a mechanism landed DARK, so no wrong identity has been served | `6dde1c2` | fixed — the finding is correct and the pattern behind it is what matters. This is the THIRD time in this slice that I asserted a dependency's shape instead of measuring it: a planted sentinel the client cannot produce, an element tag no component carries, and now an attribute that does not exist on the element that does. Each time the test agreed with the code because the test invented the same shape the code expected, so a passing test certified a path that never runs. The archive could not have settled it either — every captured customProperties block is EMPTY, so there was no populated example to read. The authority is the component builder that emits these blocks, whose own docstring records the two live components its shape was verified against. Fixed by reading the emitted attribute, and STRUCTURALLY by having the test GENERATE its fixture from that emitter, so it can no longer construct a shape the system never produces. A non-vacuity assertion guards the generated fixture too, because a generator returning an empty block would make every comparison below it pass for the wrong reason |
| QA-155-r27-01a | Revision of `QA-155-r27-01` (original retained, byte-frozen), raised by Stage-2 Codex commit review round 3, run `cdx-review.lfcEqs`, archived `commit-reviews/cdx-review.lfcEqs`, reviewed `2d51572` | Same finding; what is corrected is its DERIVED TIER | High — UNCHANGED, as the rule requires | unchanged | unchanged | **critical** — CORRECTED. The tier derives from the source label High, which this repository's tiering rule maps to critical unconditionally; the earlier derivation of standard was invalid | `2d51572` | fixed — disposition UNCHANGED; the correction is to the tier alone. The correction is the TIER, not the finding or its disposition: every one of these was already fixed and validated, so nothing was deferred and no closure rested on the wrong tier. The record was still wrong, and under this repository's own rules a derived tier that mismatches its critical anchor invalidates a closure. My justification — that the mechanism is landed dark, so no wrong verdict has been served — is a REACHABILITY argument, and the rule admits only a severity-specific technical refutation. Reachability is not one: the defect is equally real whether or not today's code path reaches it, and the slice whose whole purpose is to start reaching it is the next one. Applying that reasoning as a blanket across eight rows is worse than applying it once, because it converted a rule with a narrow escape into a default |

| CDX-155-r24-01 | Stage-2 Codex commit review round 3 (fix-only, slice B), run `cdx-review.lfcEqs`, archived `commit-reviews/cdx-review.lfcEqs`, reviewed `2d51572` | Eight audit rows record a `standard` derived tier for findings their source gate labelled P1 or High, justified by the mechanism being unreachable, which the tiering rule does not admit | P1 | machine-served schemas/contracts | a rule with a narrow escape applied as a blanket default | **critical** — derived from the source label P1, which this repository's rule maps to critical unconditionally. Tiering THIS finding standard would repeat the very error it reports | `2d51572` | fixed — all eight rows carry an `a`-revision correcting the tier to critical, originals byte-frozen, supersession map updated. The rule is explicit that a source label is immutable and yields to only a severity-specific technical refutation; mine was a REACHABILITY argument, which is a different claim — the defect is equally real whether or not today's code path reaches it, and the next slice exists to start reaching it. | CDX-155-r24-02 | Stage-2 Codex commit review round 3 (fix-only, slice B), run `cdx-review.lfcEqs`, archived `commit-reviews/cdx-review.lfcEqs`, reviewed `2d51572` | The configuration digest joins property keys with a comma, which is not injective: no keys and one empty key serialize identically, as do one key containing a comma and two keys | P2 | machine-served schemas/contracts | a digest payload built by concatenation rather than by encoding | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it P2; the mechanism is dark and no digest has been published | `2d51572` | fixed, and WIDER than reported. Both collisions were reproduced against the component builder's real output — it accepts a comma in a key, so these are reachable inputs rather than pathological ones. The same hazard sat one level up and the report did not name it: the payload was newline-joined key=value lines, so a field value containing a newline could impersonate an adjacent field. Both are replaced by a canonical encoding that represents structure instead of eliding it, and both directions are pinned — including a control that the fix did not flatten the distinctions that matter, since a digest that ignored everything would also have no collisions |
| SELF-155-r28-01 | self, found when the ledger scanner refused the tier corrections (no gate run) | Every slice-B finding row was INVISIBLE to the ledger scanner: their identifiers carried a slice segment the finding-id grammar does not accept, so twenty-seven rows were present in the file and absent from every count, tally and cross-check the scanner performs | none (self-recorded record-integrity defect) | machine-served schemas/contracts | an identifier shape invented for readability without checking the grammar that consumes it | standard — anchor: no defect shipped; the rows were correct and merely uncounted | `2d51572` | fixed — renumbered into the accepted grammar, continuing the global round sequence rather than marking the slice in the id; the slice is already recorded in each row's source column, which is where it belongs. Two capture rows of mine used a second unaccepted shape and were renamed too. This is a REPEAT: the strategy of record enumerated exactly this as a named risk before work began, and slice A hit it once already with a different segment and renamed twelve rows to clear it. I read that history, wrote a new segment, and did not test the grammar — the check costs one expression and I have now paid for it twice. Six rows from slice A remain invisible under the older shape; they are byte-frozen in a landed record and are left alone deliberately, recorded here rather than rewritten |
| CDX-155-r25-01 | Stage-2 Codex commit review round 4 (fix-only, slice B), run `cdx-review.iHqvgi`, archived `commit-reviews/cdx-review.iHqvgi`, reviewed `3fd11c1` | One of the regression tests added for the previous round's collision fix does not reproduce the defect it pins, so it would stay green through a full revert | P2 | none (a test's detection power; nothing served) | a regression case chosen by inspection rather than by running it against the code it guards | standard — anchor: source gate labelled it P2; the FIX is sound and independently pinned, so what was missing is detection of a future regression rather than the correction itself | `3fd11c1` | fixed, and the reviewer's diagnosis was exact. The old serializer sorted whole LINES, so a newline smuggled into the path sorted AFTER the field it impersonated and the two payloads already differed — my case never collided, and a passing test looked like coverage of a revert it could not survive. Verified by reconstructing the pre-fix serializer and running my case against it: distinct, exactly as reported. The replacement smuggles into the operation's TYPE attribute, which sorts before every field line, so the injected text lands where the impersonated line would have gone; a character reference is required because a literal newline in an attribute is normalised to a space while a reference is not. STRUCTURALLY, the test now carries the pre-fix serializer beside the case and ASSERTS the case collides under it, so a regression case that stops reproducing the defect fails loudly instead of passing quietly. Controlled by reverting the fix and watching the test fail, rather than by reading it |

| QA-155-r29-01 | Stage-1 QA evidence-capture dispatch E5, archives `captures/cap155-e5-patch-attested` and `captures/cap155-e5-delete-attested`, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-e5-double-attested.md` | The component list action accepts arbitrary filter keys, silently drops the ones it does not support, and returns the UNFILTERED set with a success status | High | runtime behavior | a filter honoured by one action and ignored by its sibling, failing in the direction that widens the result | **critical** — derived from the source label High, which this repository's rule maps to critical unconditionally | `6ae0fa2` | fixed — the action refuses an unsupported filter key BEFORE touching the platform, names the supported set, and points at the sibling action that does filter by name. Reproduced first: a nonexistent name returns the full unfiltered set from `list` and correctly returns nothing from `search`, so the sibling honours exactly the key whose silence made the mistake plausible. The failure direction is what makes this serious — an ignored filter returns MORE than the caller asked for, and a caller who believes the result is scoped may act destructively on it, which is precisely what happened |
| QA-155-r29-02 | Stage-1 QA evidence-capture dispatch E5, archives `captures/cap155-e5-patch-attested` and `captures/cap155-e5-delete-attested`, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-e5-double-attested.md` | A capture-round helper deleted twenty-one components it did not own, having scoped its target list with the filter key the action ignores | Critical | data loss | a destructive action taken on an unverified scope | **critical** — anchor: the blocking class is data loss, which derives critical independently of any label | `6ae0fa2` | fixed at the CAUSE rather than the instance. The deletions are irreversible and the practical impact is nil — the account is disposable and expires within hours, the twenty-one were stale artifacts of a closed issue, shared fixtures were verified intact, and the round's own evidence was already on disk. The procedure failure is real and is recorded rather than minimised: a scope was TRUSTED without being verified, and the standing rule that governs teardown covers scopes derived from an apply and says nothing about scopes derived from a query. The enabling defect is fixed at `QA-155-r29-01`; the rule's gap is recorded here so the next reader does not have to rediscover it |
| QA-155-r29-03 | Stage-1 QA evidence-capture dispatch E5, archives `captures/cap155-e5-patch-attested` and `captures/cap155-e5-delete-attested`, report `agents/reports/2026-08-27-issue-155-e5-double-attested.md` | An identity address appears unredacted in tracked evidence files | Critical | secrets/security | an identifier carried into a durable artifact by capture tooling that predates the redaction step | **critical** — anchor: the blocking class is secrets/security, which derives critical independently of any label, and the tier is recorded before any judgement about whether the value is sensitive | `6ae0fa2` | ESCALATED to the owner, not decided here. Measured: 118 occurrences across 62 tracked files, of which 64 files ARE ALREADY on the integration branch — the exposure landed with the previous slice and is not introduced by this one. The capture generations that postdate the redaction step carry ZERO occurrences, including both captures from this round, so the tooling defect is already closed going forward. What remains is a decision about existing history, and it is the owner's on two grounds: the value is their identity rather than a credential, and the remedies differ by orders of magnitude in cost and blast radius. Recorded in the secrets class because that is what the anchor requires; the tier is not a claim that the value is a secret |
| ARCH-155-B-e3-01 | §6 architect implementation review, slice B, evaluation 3 of 3 — the LAST under the tracked cap — run `cdx-gate-review.ggtsSO`, archived `architect-reviews/cdx-gate-review.ggtsSO`, attested `ok:true`, reviewed `400ada0` | Evidence rows were disconnected from the closed observations: the row carried a declared retry verdict with only a digest and execution ids, and the observation models were unused | critical | capability reachability | a verdict declared beside evidence rather than derived from it | **critical** — derived from the source label, which this repository's rule maps to critical unconditionally | `400ada0` | fixed — the row now REQUIRES its typed capture and the verdict must be one the capture's replay observation supports. Fixing it immediately caught my own classifier returning an affirmative verdict for captures whose replay was never exercised, which is the same defect one level up. A safe method being idempotent is a claim from the transport specification; this registry records what was observed |

PATCH converged with only the modification timestamp moving; DELETE is effect-idempotent but not response-idempotent, a distinction only the readback shows |

| ARCH-155-B-e3-11 | §6 architect implementation review, slice B, evaluation 3 of 3 — the LAST under the tracked cap — run `cdx-gate-review.ggtsSO`, archived `architect-reviews/cdx-gate-review.ggtsSO`, attested `ok:true`, reviewed `400ada0` | The slice's previous two architect evaluations were absent from the durable ledger and archive | closure blocker | machine-served schemas/contracts | a collected round treated as a completed one | **critical** — derived from the source label, which this repository's rule maps to critical unconditionally | `400ada0` | fixed — all three evaluations are archived with their prompts and their findings recorded. This is the most serious process failure of the slice and it was entirely mine: I ran the collector each time, read the findings, fixed them, and never archived the round. Running the collector is not completing a round. The repository's own history contains the mirror image of this — a previous issue fabricated attestations by reading findings from a poll — and I reproduced its shape from the other side: real attestations that never became evidence |
| QA-155-r29-03a | Revision of `QA-155-r29-03` (original retained, byte-frozen), raised when the owner decided | Same finding: an identity address appears unredacted in tracked evidence files | Critical | secrets/security | unchanged | **critical** — UNCHANGED; the tier derives from the blocking class and a decision not to act does not retier it | `3722fce` | fixed — RESOLVED BY OWNER DECISION: leave the existing occurrences. The later capture generations already redact, so exposure does not grow, and both captures from the final round carry zero occurrences. The part worth recording is that the decision was taken TWICE and the second answer differed. A full history rewrite was chosen first, against a one-line risk note; measuring the blast radius before acting produced numbers materially worse than that note implied — 79 commit SHAs cited by this ledger, 66 of them on the integration branch, and 27 archived rounds citing a reviewed SHA. A rewrite leaves every one unresolvable, and repointing them at the new SHAs would be WORSE than leaving them broken: the reviewer never saw the rewritten tree, so such an attestation would be the exact false claim the attestation machinery exists to refuse. Weighed against removing a company address — already ordinary in correspondence — from a private repository, the owner chose to leave it. Recorded as a DECISION, not a deferral: nothing is owed and no follow-up is filed. The lesson is about sequencing rather than about email: an irreversible action's blast radius is worth measuring BEFORE it is taken, because the measurement changed the answer |

Four rounds each added one true-but-insufficient condition — the wave SHA, then the boundary's existence, then its order, then its strictness — because I was accumulating ancestry checks from findings rather than deriving them from what the protocol specifies. The protocol specifies POSITIONS: the wave gate passes on one tree, the record edits land in the next, and the closing commit holds only an archive and prose. So the guard now asserts the chain directly — the reviewed boundary IS the commit before the tip, the wave SHA is not that commit, and it strictly precedes it. An identity leaves no room for a reflexive-reachability loophole, where a fifth ancestry clause would only have moved the gap. Graded three ways: the equality pairing fails, a stale pre-wave pairing fails, and the correct chain fails ONLY for the honest reason that its review is not yet archived |

The original row said "PARTIALLY fixed and the remainder is OPEN, carried to the terminal correction batch", and that batch closed elements and left ATTRIBUTES open. Measured before believing either way: a stray attribute planted on a projected field left the digest BYTE-IDENTICAL, which is exactly the harm the finding names — a component whose configuration changed still matching captured evidence. Now: an attribute on a projected element that is neither carried nor explicitly excluded REFUSES, the same three categories the field and element allowlists already use; `type` is carried, so a retyped routing field is a different configuration where before it was invisible; family-scoped projection lookup and the QName-aware canonicalisation arguments were already present and were verified by reading the call, not assumed. Scope is the PROJECTION, not the document: a component wrapper carries two dozen metadata attributes that never reach the digest, and refusing those would refuse every real component while proving nothing — a control test pins that. Four witnesses, all hand-run: the refusal, the metadata control, the retype moving the digest, and the whole captured corpus still digesting. `projection_version` moves to 2 because the projection changed and two digests from different projections are not comparable; the production registry has zero evidence rows, so nothing published becomes incomparable. Why it is not fixed in this slice: widening the grammar was TRIED and measured, and it surfaces 33 rows in this ledger alone that were DELETED under their original ids and re-added under scanner-visible ones — historical renames the blindness hid, spanning #153, #154, #175 and others. Reconciling those is a cross-issue record decision about whether each rename was legitimate, which is not this slice's to make and is precisely the same-slice cross-subsystem refactor the structural-fix rule forbids. What IS done here: the hazard is measured rather than asserted, the trial widening and its exact fallout are recorded so the next attempt starts from evidence, and the supersession map is left declaring exactly the revisions the scanner can see rather than gaining an entry it cannot check. The consequence worth stating plainly is that this blindness is why a critical finding sat marked OPEN through an entire closing protocol and was found by reading the ledger by hand, not by any guard |

STRUCTURAL FIX, mandatory at that recurrence: the rule is derived from the projection's own admitted-element set, which the projection already owns and already refuses a component without. Every attribute of every admitted element must be carried, explicitly excluded, or structural; nothing else passes. The classification set is finite and was MEASURED, not guessed — six attributes for a connection, nine for an operation. Carried attributes now reach the payload qualified by the element carrying them, so a change to one moves the digest; the OAuth client and endpoint attributes are excluded for the reason the credentials element already was |

It is not a coverage gap that more tests of the same shape would close. The structural answer is a guard that imports each served entry point as the FIRST module in a fresh interpreter and then touches the served state that import was meant to build. My own ledger row for `QA-155-r49-04` said the reasons should live beside the code that raises them; that was the wrong call and it is what created the cycle. QA measured the damage on the platform, reading back a created process bound to an operation whose stored path is empty with no Path property at all. The general fact this round establishes, and the reason it earned its own class instance: widening an unsettled test is ADDITIVE on a rung that refuses and SUBTRACTIVE on a rung that resolves, so one predicate serving both cannot be widened safely in either direction. Resolution and refusal are now separate signals. The regression test asserts the resolution rung THROUGH the consumer, so a later widening of the refusal cannot silence it again. Four mutants graded red including a restoration of the exact regression |

## Slice C — closing report

**Status: CLOSED for landing on the integration branch, with residue recorded and one class left
escalated. The ISSUE remains OPEN — slice C is one of six, and #155 closes only on slice F.**

### Which gate covers which tree

Three trees, named separately, because a single "the gates cover the final tree" claim is what the
closing protocol exists to prevent:

| Tree | Commit | What covers it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **W** — the wave-gated tree | `7803032` | `L4` composite wave gate, archived `wave-gate/wave155i`, exit status captured and measured as zero |
| **N−1** — the reviewed tree | `7803032` | `L2` Stage-2 commit review round 17, run `cdx-review.ckbK09`, archived, CLEAN over the complete delta since `4e300f4` |
| **N** — the closing commit | this commit | Record-only by protocol: it adds this report, the two checkpoint rows above, and the closing review's own archive. Validated by a darkness proof (no source, test or script differs between W and the tip), both ledger scanner files re-run whole, and the `scratch/**` preflight — never by a further review, which would recurse |

W and N−1 are the same commit: the wave gate ran on exactly the tree the closing review had cleared,
and the closing commit adds nothing outside `docs/architecture/`.

### Gates run, in order

| Loop | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `L1` Stage-1 QA | 13 rounds through the public MCP tool boundary on the live account, reports `agents/reports/2026-08-29..30-issue-155-slice-c-r*.md` |
| `L2` Stage-2 commit review | 17 rounds, every one collected and archived; the last returned CLEAN over the complete unreviewed delta |
| `L3` §6 architect implementation review | 3 of 3 — the tracked cap, spent; the loop ended on a clean commit review as that rule prescribes |
| `L4` composite wave gate | 1 round, PASS on `W` |
| `L5` closing protocol | this report |

### What this slice built, and what it does NOT yet do

The trusted connector-resolution snapshot and the blank-path cross-check are built, and a caller's
declared connector family and action can no longer override what the component's own bytes resolve
to on any route that builds a symbol table. Live QA confirmed the refusal by name on the reuse,
reference-only, raw-create, raw-update and collision-reuse routes, and confirmed the blank-path
refusal and its bound control.

Recorded plainly because a closing report that overstates is the defect this ledger keeps catching:
**two of the surfaces this slice hardened have no production consumer today.** The pre-parsed live
projection channel is populated by no caller, and the projected endpoint field is read by nothing —
its `mintable` derivation is referenced nowhere and the snapshot is never serialised into a tool
response. Several late corrections therefore closed real defects on a surface production does not
observe. They are correct and worth landing, because slices D and E are the consumers the plan
designs them for, but no claim is made here that they improved what a caller sees.

### Residue carried out of this slice

- `QA-155-r55-04a` — **ESCALATED OPEN, not deferred.** The derivation pinning a composer's declared
  fields to its body matches only double-quoted reads. It joins the residue already enumerated under
  the `DC-155-D` escalation; no follow-up issue is filed because filing is the owner's to authorise,
  and the record does not claim a deferral it cannot meet.
- The closing-chain guard checks a marker naming slice B only, so it does not cover this report.
  Widening it would be a further structural attempt at `DC-155-D`, which that class's escalation
  forbids. The chain was therefore verified explicitly instead, and the evidence is the table above:
  W named and archived, N−1 named and attested by an archived clean review, W strictly preceding N,
  and the darkness proof over source, tests and scripts.

### Restoration

None used. No provisional correction was ever left unvalidated at HEAD.

## Slice D — Stage-1 step 0 — baseline

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Literal baseline SHA | `d04a2482d9b43c84765395ec8bbc73495de29fd4` |
| Branch point | `dev` @ `d04a248` (slice C's landed closing commit) |
| Baseline suite | 11,235 passed / 18 skipped, tree clean |
| Slice kind | behaviour-affecting — per-call replay grants, the shared reference grammar, candidate discovery, and the account-independent semantic revision (plan §5.D, U6 + U7) |
| Live account | `trainingglebbochkarov-16926N`, active |
| Trust boundary | CREATES the grant symbols and their minter, the authored reference grammar, the discovery action and its diagnostic, and the semantics revision row; CONSUMES the compiler's own lowering and binding resolution, the connector registry built in slice B, and the trusted snapshot built in slice C |

**Recorded at step 0, before the rest of the slice — the step-0 block itself is LATE.** The
re-keying batch below was applied and validated before this table was written, which inverts the
order the ledger discipline prescribes. Recorded rather than backdated: the batch's own evidence is
intact and independently checkable, and the cost of the inversion is that this table was not
available to the batch it describes.

### Batch 1 — the contract re-keying

Applied as ONE batch because the plan requires it: uniqueness, sort, the index and both consumers
move together, and a partial change makes accept-versus-refuse depend on authoring order. Keyed on
`(ref, operation_ref)` rather than the reference alone — a contract binds a reference to the one
operation it covers, so the same reference covering two operations is two contracts, not a
duplicate. Resolution is now a single lookup on the pair the index is keyed by, replacing a fetch
followed by a separate comparison that a later edit could drop.

Validation: the full non-KB suite at 11,237 passed / 18 skipped, and both reverts hand-run against
the real pre-change text — restoring ref-only uniqueness and restoring the ref-only index each fail
the capability test, with the restores digest-verified.

### Batch 2 — the authored reference grammar

The rule now lives in `connector_replay.ids`, beside the repository's other identifier grammars and
importing nothing from the compiler or the categories layer. Both consumers ask it: the authoring
model that ACCEPTS a document and the compiler symbol that RESOLVES one. It was hand-coded in the
model before, and permissive enough to accept a further-structured value carrying extra colons. The
refusal has its own registered code, owner `#155`, because the generic component-reference code
cannot say which of two different grammars was broken — a component reference also admits a literal
id, a contract reference never does.

**Measured, and it contradicts the plan's prescribed form.** §5.D specifies
`StringConstraints(pattern=…)` beside a root-owned `BeforeValidator`. On pydantic 2.12.3 that
combination serves NO pattern at all: a before-validator makes the constraint apply to the
validator's OUTPUT, which JSON Schema cannot express, so the constraint is dropped from the served
document without an error. Written as prescribed, the grammar would have been invisible to every
machine reading the contract while the tests still passed. The pattern is therefore carried as
schema metadata read from the same constant the validator asks, and a test pins the served pattern
EQUAL to the enforced one so the two cannot drift.

Served delta, keyed by entry id rather than by list position: exactly ONE added authoring-contract
entry, for the new diagnostic — nothing removed, nothing changed in place. Recorded because the
first diff of this rebaseline was index-keyed and reported 2,153 moved leaves, which reads as a
wholesale reordering; a single insertion had shifted every later index. Rebaselined: the ProcessIR
schema (one `pattern` key, `$defs` unchanged at 40), the authoring contract, and the M12.12
inventory. Validation: the full non-KB suite at 11,243 passed / 18 skipped, with both mutants
hand-run — dropping the compiler symbol's check while the authoring surface stays strict fails the
lockstep pin, and widening the authority to admit further colons fails the named-code negatives.

### Batch 3 — per-call replay grants

Shapes, minter and consumer land TOGETHER, deliberately. The plan's own risk list names a field
nothing populates as the vacuity trap this epic has hit twice, and a grant symbol without a minter
or a minter without a checker would be exactly that.

What it changes: evidence moves from per-OPERATION to per-CALL. A contract binds a reference to the
operation it covers, so before this a second call of the same operation elsewhere in the same root
inherited evidence nobody minted for it. The grant names the call site, and on a root-projected
table the retry check requires one for the very call it is examining. A GRANT-FREE table — one with
no projected root — still compiles: that is the honest pre-projection state that effect-declaration
resolution runs in, and demanding a grant there would refuse a document for lacking evidence about a
call nobody has been asked about yet.

**A defect in my own first version, recorded because the tests would not have caught it.** The
minter read the authored evidence off the connector-call binding, where it does not live. It
therefore minted NOTHING for every input, silently, while every other test stayed green — an empty
result is indistinguishable from a minter that is never exercised. It now reads the CFG node, which
is the same authority the retry check reads, and the mutant restoring the original form fails the
non-vacuity test. A second correction of the same kind: a negative test asserted that a read-only
call ignores a dangling contract reference. Measured, it does not — an unresolvable reference is
refused whichever row it sits on — so the test states the true negative instead, with the correction
kept in its own text.

Call paths come from the compiler's own binding resolution rather than a private walk, and
enumeration failure mints nothing and raises nothing: an unresolvable reference is the validator's
finding, and reporting it from the minter too would report one defect from two layers.

Validation: the full non-KB suite at 11,247 passed / 18 skipped. Mutants hand-run — reading evidence
off the binding mints nothing and fails the minter pin; removing the grant check loses call-site
discrimination; consulting grants on a grant-free table fails five tests.

### Batch 4 — candidate discovery

A new action on the component-query tool answers one question: for a named operation and connection,
does the packaged registry hold a record whose identity matches what the account stores now. Every
answer is a CANDIDATE and says so in the payload — `authority` and `candidate_only` are fields
rather than prose someone has to remember — because the compiler decides sufficiency and this
surface only says which reference is worth naming. An empty list is a real answer: no record for a
pair is the normal state until evidence is ingested, and reporting it as a failure would make the
absence of evidence look like a broken read. An unreadable identity fails closed AND names which of
the two components could not be read.

The served field set is CLOSED by construction — the projection names what it emits — and its test
asserts EQUALITY against the declared set rather than containment. The underlying record carries a
capture reference and a route coverage naming a request path; the test plants both and proves
neither reaches the output, keeping only the coverage KIND.

**The action list is now derived rather than transcribed.** It was hand-written in the router's
error hint and again in the served capability catalogue. This repository has already been bitten by
that exact shape — the catalogue advertised ten monitoring actions where the router accepted
seventeen — so one tuple on the router is the authority and both served copies read it.

**A registered code with no producer, caught by an existing guard.** The repository requires every
published replay code to be producible, on the reasoning that a caller branching on a code nothing
emits has written dead error handling. Discovery reports by RETURNING a served envelope rather than
raising, so the code was registered and unproduced. The guard now checks production rather than only
raising, and exercises the discovery path for real with an identity the account cannot read — the
property it defends is unchanged; what was too narrow was its idea of how a code reaches a caller.

Rebaselined: the M12.12 inventory and its §11 markdown, for the served-surface edits. Validation:
the full non-KB suite at 11,256 passed / 18 skipped.
